The determination's argument: f11 L161 s3 makes appropriateness "part of the claim" and f11 L514 makes it a "stated input" on which the verdict is "a verdict given the input." Greta's claim states it (yeast works slowly in cold), so the input is present. The verdict given it is that the restriction is appropriate.

The semicolon separates two cases: (1) work supplied from outside = outside contribution; (2) a process that runs inside = the system's own, whoever wrote it. The uploaded routine: the *process* runs inside → the system's own, whoever wrote it. The *writing* was outside → the origin is the person's. Both halves of the fixed verdict! The clause "whoever wrote it" directly addresses O30's situation. So the determination's reading is textually strong: "whoever written it" only makes sense if an externally-written, internally-run process is the system's own.

File 10's (P) evaluates r at ξ and ξ' — endpoints. The formula r(ξ)⇒r(ξ') only looks at endpoints. "## Scope, and a question that can be wrong
A contract is a declared subset of the physically admitted changes, and a stated scope is what makes it one. An account at a stated scope answers the question asked at that scope; it does not answer a broader question that failed, and a narrowing adopted after a failure is a new claim at a new index (Part VIII). What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it. A question may fail to pick out its alleged target..."

Hmm. Here's a cleaner way to think about it. The mark rule: "The theory's verdict. What the theory, read as it stands, finds on each point, by its own definitions and conditions. Where the theory leaves a word undefined, read it in its ordinary sense."

Point: "This is a legitimate narrowing" — the finding: the narrowing counts as legitimate.

The theory's definitions and conditions: L161 s3 defines appropriateness (a substantive, criticizable part of the claim) and refuses certification. So the theory's verdict on "is it legitimate?" — the theory's condition for legitimacy is "a substantive, criticizable part of the claim." Greta's claim has one (the yeast account). Hmm, alternatively the sentence means: the semantics records the restriction (and its stated ground) and does not certify the restriction's appropriateness at all — i.e., the semantics reaches no verdict of legitimacy. Hmm. "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim" — "What makes X is Y" = "Y is what makes X" = Y constitutes/makes X. So: the semantics doesn't *certify* the restriction. But the appropriateness-maker is identified. Hmm.

So the question: is L161 s3's first clause a *condition* (generating a verdict) or a *remark* (locating appropriateness)? "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim" — grammatically, this is a *constitutive* claim: it says what appropriateness consists in. Constitutive claims generate verdicts (via the case's facts). E.g., "What makes a shape a triangle is having three sides" — given a shape with three sides, it's a triangle.

So "criticizable" is a global caveat, not a withholding. → Same finding.
